環保法規，須負擔高昂翻修成本，使法國房價呈現連三季下滑所拖累。 
 近一週法國 10 年期公債殖利率升至 4.9%以上，與德國公債殖利率差距擴大
至 125 個基點以上，主要反映市場對法國主權公債可持續性疑慮升溫。儘管目前
政府計畫將 2027 年財政赤字縮限至 5%，惟政府在國會並無穩定多數席次，即使
能以行政命令強行通過預算 ， 也可能面臨倒閣風險 ；此外 ，市場對於2027 年 4 月
總統大選後，新總統能否取得國會足夠支持，推動削減赤字與控制債務的改革亦
缺乏信心。因此未來法國仍可能面臨殖利率進一步上升與財政壓力加劇的風險，
暫時不利於投資及消費信心。 
三、日本—景氣溫和復甦，但消費放緩與通膨升溫風險升高 
 8 月工業生產月減 1.7%，主要係受到該月地震及豪雨等天然災害影響，導致
部分工廠暫時停產。從細項來看，汽車業受停工影響最為明顯，產量下滑6.8%，
電子產品產量亦減少 1.9%。 惟生產機械產量連續三個月成長 ， 加上核心資本財出
貨持續增加，顯示企業投資需求仍相當強勁，有助於支撐後續工業生產回升。從
企業預期來看，製造商預期9 月產量將成長 3.2%，顯示生產活動可望迅速回溫，
在供應鏈逐步恢復、庫存回補需求持續，以及穩健的企業資本支出支撐下，預計
第三季工業生產仍可維持正成長，且成長動能可望延續至年底。 
 8 月零售月減 1.2%，雖然表現略優於市場預期，但仍顯示民間消費動能正在
放緩。此次疲弱主要原因來自耐久財銷售下滑，其中家電銷售因空調等產品在換
機需求告一段落後明顯回落，汽車銷售也隨政府刺激措施效應逐漸消退而連續第
四個月下降。相較之下，食品、服裝等非耐久財銷售雖同步走弱，但降幅相對有
限 ， 反映薪資成長仍對消費提供部分支撐 。惟不利天候與通膨壓力仍是主要風險 ，
8 月至 9 月持續豪雨已影響服裝等非必需消費，而除米價以外的食品及商品價格
持續上漲，也正侵蝕家庭實質購買力，恐限制消費者信心與消費復甦力道。 
 9 月東京核心通膨年增率升至 2.7%，雖然東京都免費托兒及免費水費政策造
成的基期效應貢獻約 0.5 個百分點，但通膨升溫主因仍是價格上漲已由能源與食
品擴散至整體經濟。受石油價格上揚帶動，企業開始加速將原物料及薪資成本轉
嫁給消費者，帶動商品與服務價格普遍走高；租金通膨更升至 2%，創 1994 年以
來新高，公共交通及醫療等項目也開始調漲。隨著東京的物價壓力逐步反映至全
國數據，日本央行進一步升息的可能性升高。

四、中國—為刺激內需、引導產業走向，人行漸近式放寬政策工具 
9 月官方製造業 PMI 上升 0.3 點至 50.1，為近三個月高點，新訂單、出口訂
單指數分別為 50.5、50，並帶動生產指數上升至 51.7，不過，庫存指數下降、就
業指數加速衰退、企業展望減弱，皆拖累 PMI 指數水準。此外，投入、產出物價
指數分別上升至 60.8、54，顯示成本壓力加劇，但售價調整速度依然較為遲緩，
不利於獲利表現。官方非製造業 PMI 上升 1.2 點至 50.2，其中建築業活動指數大
增 3.4 點至 50.3，脫離連續八個月的衰退區間，主要是政府加大財政刺激力度，
推動重大建設開工；非製造業的從業人員指數為 45.9，顯示就業情況仍顯嚴峻，
亦較不利於內需表現。 
鑒於內需動能疲弱，人行於 9 月 29 日宣布下調抵押補充貸款(該貸款本質是
將資金引導至政府希望發展的領域)利率自 1.75%降至 1.50%，並擴大適用範圍，
將水網、新型電網、算力網、新一代通信網、城市地下管網及物流網等建設納入
支持，有助於提振內需動能。未來若內需復甦力道不如預期，不排除人行將進一
步降準或擴大結構性貨幣工具支持經濟；至於全面降息則仍須兼顧人民幣匯率及
中美利差等因素，可能性相對較低。 
五、印度及東南亞—泰國、菲律賓出口均達 2 成以上的高速成長 
(1)新加坡：受服務業、零售暨其他商品業，以及食品價格漲幅擴大影響，8 月消
費者物價指數(CPI)年增率升至 2.3%、不包括住宿及個人交通費用之核心 CPI 年
增率升至 2.2%， 連續3 個月上升並創 2 年新高 。 受全球能源價格居高不下及氣候
條件不佳影響，預期新加坡進口食品、商品及服務成本將持續上升，惟國內生產
率成長及政府補貼政策將抑制部分服務業通膨，新加坡金管局(MAS)或在 10 月
「略微」緊縮貨幣政策。(2)泰國：8 月出口額年增 24.3%(346 億美元)，主要由工
業產品出口成長 27.1%所帶動，其中電腦及相關設備（年增 42.1%）、電話及零
件（151.2%） 及電路板 （19.2%） 增幅最為顯著，對美國出口年增48.7%、對中國
和日本分別成長 15.5%、19.6%。8 月進口額年增 25.1%(371 億美元)，以燃料 （年
增 42.8%） 、 資本貨物 （33.9%） 、 其他貨物 （27.4%） 、 原材料和中間產品 （21.3%）、
運輸設備（13.6%）為主。進出口相抵，8 月貿易逆差 24.8 億美元，較 7 月略收
窄，為連續第 11 個月出現逆差。(3)菲律賓：8 月出口額大增 27.8%(91 億美元)，

 投資評價與建議 
目標價維持 320 元：京元電短期逆風未平，然 2027 年將迎來強勁成長，係考量
(1) 年底為 CPU、網通客戶等增加的產能，可自 1Q27 起完整貢獻營收，(2) 京元
電有機會增加在主要客戶 TPU 的市占率，目前已在和客戶討論下世代晶片搭配的
預燒爐，(3) HPC 新品放量，採用液冷預燒爐帶動測試單價提升。惟須留意產能快
速擴張下的成本增加。評價面，以2027 年修正後 EPS 推算目前 PER 約 23.3x，考
量 AI/HPC 相關需求未見軟弱，建議拉回再行佈局，目標價 320 元(25.2x 2027 
EPS(F))。

平均得分分別為 B+等與 B+等。

### Page 2

2

京元電為國內 IC 測試大廠：成立於 1987 年，主要提供前段晶圓測試(CP)及後段
IC 成品測試(FT)、預燒等服務，近年透過自製測試設備增加取得 AI 客戶信任，同
時降低封裝業務來改善公司平均獲利能力。 2Q26 營收產品組合為晶圓測試
29.6%、產品測試 61%、產品預燒 7.7%、封裝 1.3%、其他 0.4%。應用別營收比
重為消費性電子 30.3%、通訊 17.5%、數據處理與儲存 36.3%、汽車電子 13.0%、
工業 2.5%、其他 0.4%。目前主要生產據點位於桃園楊梅、苗栗竹南、苗栗頭份、
苗栗銅鑼廠區，並正在新加坡、美國等地進行佈局。主要客戶包含全球主要半導體
公司，如聯發科、韋爾半導體、AMD(Xilinx)、意法半導體等，主要競爭對手為日
月光、矽格等。 
美國廠放量或在 2028 年後 ：研究部預期台積電美國先進封裝廠區將於 2H28-2029
年進入量產，京元電尚未公布美國廠的具體時程與選址，但我們認為 AeternZ 廠
址存在落腳德州的可能性，理由有三：(1) 下游組裝廠已在當地佈局，(2) 亞利桑
那州須與晶圓廠爭搶技術人力，(3) 京元電位處後段測試，靠近終端組裝較符合供
應鏈配置。另外，本次公告的客戶預付款，我們認為宣示意義大於實質挹注，係因
封測廠資本支出以設備為大宗 ， 而該客戶的測試設備多已採託管(consignment)模
式，京元電本身需投入的設備資金不高，預付款對降低資本支出壓力的效果有限。
此外 ，京元電赴美設廠短期內本就以服務少數關鍵客戶為主 ， 此公告屬預期內的進
展，我們不建議過度解讀。 
來 料 不 如 預 期 ， 下 修 短 期 展 望 ：研 究 部 下修 京 元 電4Q26 營收至 131 億
(+8.4%QoQ，+32.0%YoY， 原估143 億)， 主要考量(1) 上游來料不順 ， 造成AI/HPC
相關產品放量幅度不如預期，而對應的 CPU 出貨則穩定墊高，(2) 客戶下修非旗
艦機款封測需求。獲利部分同樣因營收放大不如預期、新產能開出，抵銷掉少了夏
季電費的正向影響，預估毛利率 39.2%，稅後淨利 29.8 億(+14.9%QoQ，
+32.8%YoY)，EPS 2.32 元。 
2027 年展新機：目前我們並未見到 AI/HPC 終端需求轉弱的跡象，因此京元電業
績拉升僅是時間問題，預期 2027 年受惠(1) 年底為 CPU、網通等客戶增加的產
能，可自 1Q27 起完整貢獻營收，(2) 京元電有機會增加在主要客戶 TPU 的市站
率，目前已在和客戶討論下世代晶片搭配的預燒爐，(3) HPC 新品放量，採用液冷
預燒爐帶動測試單價提升。從供給端來看，楊梅廠、頭份一廠、頭份二廠的無塵室
在 1H27 陸續開出後，我們認為2027 年京元電的成長將不再受到產能限制，而是
看客戶的拉貨節奏。 
財務預估：研究部預估京元電 2026 年營收 466 億(+33.5%YoY)，毛利率 39.1%，
稅後淨利 103.5 億(-6.0%YoY，年減主因 2025 年公司處分蘇州廠房)，EPS 8.07
元(原估 8.81 元)。2027 年營收 682 億(+46.3%YoY)，毛利率 41.1%，稅後淨利
163.2 億(+57.6%YoY)，EPS 12.72 元(原估 13.93 元)。

MACRO STRATEGY 
MARKET VIEW 
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK 
MARKETS IN REVIEW 
Portfolio Considerations
We view recent volatility as a rolling
correction rather than the end of
the cycle. We favor diversified Equity

exposure, with potential opportunities
extending beyond Growth to Value,
Small- and Mid-caps, Non-U.S. Equities
(especially Emerging Markets), dividend-
focused and thematic strategies.
We favor adjusting portfolios toward
strategic durations given attractive
elevated nominal and real yields, and see
value in high-quality bonds, particularly
longer-dated municipal bonds.
Consider broader portfolio
diversification with potential
opportunities across industrial metals,

energy, Real/Tangible Assets, Hedge
Funds, private markets and global
strategies to capture themes from AI,
reindustrialization, infrastructure growth
and market dispersion.
All data, projections and opinions are as of the date of this report and subject to change.
I
N THIS  ISSUE
Macro Strategy—The  Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran: Bessent’s Three-Front War and
the Stakes for
Investors: Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury secretary, but
these days he looks more like America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches

from T
okyo, where he is trying to engineer a stronger Japan yen, to the U.S. Treasury
market, where he is battling to keep the 10-year yield from breaking  decisively above
5%, and to T
ehran, where he has become the point man in the escalating U.S. campaign
to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Ho
w Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A decisive victory on
all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets is next to impossible,

while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best laid plans. That said, however,
the U.S. Secretary of the Treasury has signaled that he is ready to take a very  proactive,
pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less encouraging news stems
from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the Treasury secretary.
Market View—Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”: Behind
ever
y Artificial Intelligence (AI)-generated response is a vast physical network of data

centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems, semiconductor facilities
and critical-mineral supply chains. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power,
water, advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and
intensify pressure
on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend
beyon
d AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
Thought of the Week—The Beauty of September: Call it an eventful first 13 trading

days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per barrel, diesel prices surpassed
$6
per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S. Treasury yield breached 5%, and
the Federal Reserve (Fed) joined the European Central Bank (ECB) and the Bank of Japan
(BoJ) in hiking rates for the
first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has
shed 1.4% since the start of the month. It’s not unexpected that September would be
choppy for U.S. Equities, especially in a midterm election year. The good news is that

this has historically been an attractive entry point. Since 1950, the S&P 500 has never
been
negative in the six or twelve months post-midterm election, boasting average
returns of 13.8% and 15.8%, respectively. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election. A correction amid a strong fundamental economic backdrop
would be a
potential buying opportunity in our view.

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Yen, 10-Year Yield and Iran:  Bessent’s Three-Front War and the Stakes for Investors
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Scott Bessent may carry the title of Treasury Secretary, but these days he looks more like
America’s financial field marshal. His battlefield stretches from Tokyo, where he is trying
to engineer a stronger Japan yen; to the U.S. Treasury market, where he is battling to keep

the 10-year yield from breaking decisively above 5%; and Tehran, where he has become the
point man in the escalating U.S. campaign to squeeze Iran financially and economically.
Make no mistake about it: Secretary Bessent and the Treasury Department are increasingly
an instrument of American economic statecraft, charged not simply with financing the
government and overseeing the financial system, but with managing markets, currencies
and geopolitical adversaries simultaneously. Welcome to the new age of financial warfare.

Front One: Defending the Yen. The first front unfolded over the summer when Secretary
Bessent and the BoJ stealthily intervened in support of the yen, which had fallen to a near
40-year low against the dollar by late July. To arrest the yen’s decline, the U.S. Treasury sold
euros instead of dollars in support of the yen, while pushing the BoJ to make use of the
Fed’s emergency repo facilities. In further support of the yen, the BoJ did what Bessent and

the markets have been pining for, raising its official rate by 25 basis points (bps) to 1.25%.
Since intervening in the foreign exchange markets, the yen has appreciated by nearly 5%
against the U.S. dollar, alleviating concerns of a disorderly unwind of the yen and giving
Bessent—thus far—an important victory. Defending the yen, of course, isn’t simply about
Japan—it’s about the U.S. and the need for foreign capital inflows.
Japan is one of America’s largest foreign creditors. A persistently weak yen creates

pressure on Tokyo to defend its currency, potentially by selling some of its enormous
holdings of foreign assets, including U.S. Treasurys. That prospect didn’t sit well with the
Treasury secretary. At precisely the moment Washington needs foreign capital to finance
its enormous fiscal deficits, the last thing Mr. Bessent wants is one of America’s largest
creditors to become a significant seller of Treasury securities. Hence, the battle to backstop

Japan’s currency.
Front Two: Defending the 10-Year Bond. Mr. Bessent has had less success on the
second front: the U.S. bond market. While the 10-year Treasury yield slipped slightly
following last week’s decision by the Fed to boost the fed funds rate by 25 bps, the
benchmark interest rate is up roughly 85 bps since the start of the year. Stubbornly high
inflation, elevated oil prices, enormous government borrowing requirements and growing
investor anxiety over America’s fiscal trajectory have all converged and conspired to keep

the 10-year yield closer to 5% than 4%.
Field Marshal Bessent is very aware that the 10-year Treasury is the gravitational center of
global finance—and that mortgage rates, corporate borrowing costs, equity valuations and
the government’s own interest bill all take their cue from it. A sustained move materially
above 5% would tighten financial conditions across the American economy, potentially
threaten the Equity bull market, and pile more pressure on the Republican Party.

To prevent this from happening, the Treasury has expanded its bond-buyback operations,
including purchases of longer-dated government securities, in an effort to improve liquidity
and relieve some pressure on the market, while shifting new government debt issuance
into short-term bills. Yet the bond vigilantes have so far proved harder to intimidate than
currency traders. Even after Treasury announced additional purchases, yields continued
climbing before receiving some relief from the latest hawkish move from the Fed.

Over the medium term, if investors believe Washington is borrowing too much, inflation is
running too hot or the supply of bonds is overwhelming global demand, they will demand a
higher yield to own America’s debt. This battle is far from over.
Front Three: Squeezing Tehran. Then there is Iran, where Mr. Bessent is wielding an
entirely different weapon: America’s control over the plumbing of global finance. In practice
Investment Implications
We expect continued investor

support and preference for real hard
assets, and a bias towards a plethora
of commodities and industrial plays.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 21, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
this means the Treasury can make a foreign bank choose between doing business
with Iran or the U.S. It can freeze assets, blacklist companies, restrict transactions and
pressure allies to close financial escape routes.
Under “Operation Economic Outcast,” billed as economic “D-Day,” Treasury has
intensified sanctions against Iranian entities and financial networks, including recent

actions against Iran aviation and additional efforts aimed at banks and organizations
facilitating Tehran’s international transactions. Mr. Bessent has described the objective
in unusually stark terms: to “economically asphyxiate” the Iran regime. This is economic
warfare in its purest form, but whether it works remains to be seen.
“Asphyxiating” an economy takes time and patience—and international cooperation
and coordination. It means closing multiple global loopholes of third-party entities still

willing and able to do business with Iran—look no further than Russian banks. In addition,
squeezing a major oil producer runs the risk of aggravating global oil supplies, keeping
oil prices elevated. Higher energy costs, in turn, feed right back into the U.S. inflation
outlook the Fed is trying to tame and keeps the bond vigilantes on guard and ready to
push back. Not unlike the battle to suppress U.S. bond yields, the battle to bring Iran to
heel is going to be a long one.

How does it all end? How Scott Bessent’s three-front war plays out remains to be seen. A
decisive victory on all three fronts is unlikely since corralling currency and bond markets
is next to impossible, while the whims of geopolitics can lay waste to the best-laid plans.
That said, however, the U.S. Treasury Secretary has signaled that he is ready to take
a very proactive, pro-growth approach to his job. That’s the good news. The less
encouraging news stems from the inconsistencies in the overarching objectives of the

Treasury secretary.
Mr. Bessent can pressure Iran because access to the dollar-based financial system
remains extraordinarily valuable. He can intervene in foreign-exchange markets because
America’s financial resources give Washington enormous clout. And he can attempt to
influence the Treasury market because U.S. government securities remain the foundation
of the global financial system. But therein lies the contradiction.
The U.S. is simultaneously weaponizing the financial system abroad while becoming more

dependent on that same financial system at home. Washington wants the world to fear
losing access to dollars while also asking the world to lend it trillions of dollars. It wants to
use Treasury and dollar dominance as instruments of geopolitical coercion while running
fiscal deficits that require foreigners to continue believing deeply in those very assets.
The more Washington borrows, the more sensitive it becomes to bond-market discipline.
The more aggressively it weaponizes access to the dollar, the greater the incentive for

adversaries to seek alternatives. And the more the Treasury secretary intervenes directly
in markets, the more investors may begin wondering where economic policy ends and
market management begins.

For the moment, Mr. Bessent is fighting on three fronts. He wants a stronger yen, a
lower 10-year Treasury yield and an economically isolated Iran. Success on any one front
would be difficult. Achieving all three simultaneously will require considerable skill—and
perhaps a little luck.

MARKET VIEW
Beyond AI’s Resource Footprint, Consider its “Handprint”
Anna Potts, CFA, Vice President and Investment Strategist
Since the launch of ChatGPT, investors have celebrated AI’s potential while growing
more concerned about its resource footprint: Behind every AI-generated response is
a physical network of data centers, power plants, transmission lines, cooling systems,

semiconductor facilities and critical-mineral supply chains. No doubt, the AI revolution
has environmental costs. As adoption accelerates, growing demand for power, water,
advanced hardware and raw materials could strain grids, raise emissions and intensify
pressure on local resources. Yet the sustainability conversation should extend beyond
AI’s footprint to consider whether its environmental benefits can outweigh its
resource demands.
AI’s footprint is large, growing and certainly not trivial. AI increases electricity demand,

adds large, concentrated loads to already constrained grids, and may slow the retirement
of polluting energy assets. U.S. data centers could account for 20% of national electricity
consumption by 2035, up from 5.9% today, with power needs reaching 194 gigawatts
(comparable to the electricity demand of more than 150 million homes). 1 Cooling, power
generation and semiconductor manufacturing require water; each new megawatt also
embeds substantial copper, aluminum and other materials; and short hardware cycles

can increase electronic waste. Use varies sharply by workload: Complex reasoning and
agentic tasks can consume many times more energy than a simple text prompt. The
effects are already reaching consumers and communities. Wholesale power costs on
PJM—the largest U.S. grid—rose 76% year-over-year in Q1 2026, while data-center
load included in its previous two capacity auctions added an estimated $13.8 billion to
customer bills. 2 Communities are mobilizing around their concerns, including affordability,

water use and air pollution: Q1 2026 produced the largest single quarterly concentration
of delayed or blocked data-center projects on record. 3 Opposition is therefore becoming
a development constraint to be considered.
Context, caveats and comparisons. Headlines often tell only part of the story. Globally,
data centers account for about 1% of electricity use, 4 modest relative to many industries,
and 9% of estimated global 2025 to 2030 electricity growth. 5 A typical text prompt on

one major company’s AI model 6 uses 0.24 watt-hours of electricity, emits 0.03 grams
of CO₂ and consumes 0.26 milliliters of water—roughly 617,000 prompts match the
emissions from producing and cooking one sirloin steak, while 262,000 match the water
used in an eight-minute shower. 7 Still, billions of interactions, more complex workloads
and growth in resource-constrained regions can create meaningful cumulative impacts.
The most meaningful assessment therefore depends on workload, utilization, location,

grid mix, cooling design, local water availability and who bears the cost of supporting
infrastructure, not one universal number.
AI’s Handprint. While much remains to be seen, AI’s environmental “handprint”—the
benefits it can enable—could potentially prove larger than its direct footprint in some
areas. Across the energy system, AI can help utilities better match electricity supply
and demand, balance grids, optimize storage and reduce wasted energy. Data centers

already account for more than 30% of corporate renewable power-purchase agreements,
helping create long-dated demand for clean generation. 8 Beyond power, AI can improve
precision agriculture, streamline transportation and inventories, and support product

investment in power generation,

advanced cooling, water treatment
and reuse, efficient computing, and

strategies may offer exposure to
these physical enablers while reducing
reliance on benchmark names.

design, predictive maintenance, waste sorting and recycling. AI is transforming climate
risk management by using satellite imagery, weather data and predictive analytics to
forecast floods, wildfires and extreme weather events, enabling governments, companies,
and investors to anticipate risks and allocate capital more effectively (Exhibit 2). These
gains are not automatic: they depend on innovation, adoption, and whether efficiency

improvements reduce total resource use rather than simply stimulate more demand. All
industries are faced with the opportunity to responsibly use AI to make operations more
efficient, and much is yet to be discovered.

AI’s potential handprint becomes more tangible in transportation, agriculture and climate
resilience. In aviation, for example, AI can combine weather forecasts, flight paths and
atmospheric conditions to reroute aircraft around regions where warming contrails
are most likely to form—reducing climate impact without requiring a new aircraft or

fuel system. On farms, machine learning can interpret aerial imagery and field-level
sensor data to identify crop stress early, directing water, fertilizer and chemicals to
where they are needed and helping growers improve yields while reducing inputs. AI
can also strengthen climate preparedness by analyzing satellite imagery, weather data
and infrastructure exposure to detect deforestation, anticipate floods or wildfires,
and identify communities, facilities and supply chains most vulnerable to physical

hazards. These are just a few examples that illustrate how AI’s value can extend beyond
incremental efficiency gains to reshape real-world decisions.
Investment Considerations. The challenges created by AI also create potential
investment opportunities. Companies are investing in efficient chips, closed-loop cooling,
reclaimed-water systems, energy storage, grid upgrades and cleaner firm power to
reduce resource intensity and improve reliability. Professionally managed environmental

and thematic strategies can target these “picks and shovels” of the digital buildout,
alongside businesses using AI to improve efficiency across energy management,
water infrastructure, environmental services, waste management and compute. A
disciplined thematic approach can diversify exposure beyond the largest benchmark
constituents while positioning for structural growth in power demand, grid modernization
and resource efficiency. Focusing solely on AI’s footprint risks overlooking both the

companies helping it scale more efficiently and the broader environmental benefits its
handprint may enable.

THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The Beauty of September
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Call it an eventful first 13 trading days of September: Brent crude re-eclipsed $100 per
barrel, diesel prices surpassed $6 per gallon for the first time in history, the 10-year U.S.
Treasury yield breached 5%, 9 and the Fed joined the ECB and the BoJ in hiking rates for

the first time since July 2023. Through it all, the S&P 500 has shed 1.4% since the start
of the month, while its equal-weighted counterpart is down by 3.3%.
It’s not all too unexpected that September would be a choppy month for U.S. Equities.
Historical seasonal patterns suggest as much. Since 1928, the average S&P 500 return
in September has come out to -1.1%, 10 not only the worst month of the year on average
but also the only month when the benchmark has ended lower more often than it’s

ended higher. Similar weakness has been seen in midterm election years, with the S&P
500 producing an average September return of -0.8% since 1950. 10
The good news is that this has historically been an attractive entry point for U.S. Equity
investors. Call it the beauty of September: In contrast to September’s average decline,
average returns improve in October (+3.0%), November (+2.7%), and December (+0.8%).
Indeed, the S&P 500 has never been negative in the six or 12 months post-midterm

election since 1950 (Exhibit 4A), boasting an impressive average return of 13.8% and
15.8% over those respective periods. 10
Looking ahead to the coming weeks, we have little reason to believe that 2026
will diverge from the historical trend. Volatility is likely to rise into October before
moderating post-election, in line with what’s typical in the months surrounding the
midterms (Exhibit 4B). A quieter corporate calendar ahead of Q3 earnings means

investor focus will remain on incoming macro data, the path of energy prices, and the
Fed’s policy path.
In the meantime, while the S&P 500 still stands just 2.7% below its mid-August high,
churn beneath the surface already appears to be taking place. Just 38% of S&P 500
members now stand above their 50-day moving average versus 70% a mere month ago. 9
Far from worrisome, a correction in the near term in the context of a strong fundamental
economic backdrop would be a potential buying opportunity in our view.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

Economy 
COLA comfort for older consumers? 
27 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Older generations' spending growth has lagged other cohorts over the last year in Bank of America aggregated credit and debit 
card data, despite sizeable wealth gains. This likely partly reflects the highly uneven distribution of wealth among older 
households, limiting the extent to which rising wealth supports spending across the cohort. 
• More recently, stronger consumer spending appears to be primarily a labor-income story. However, older households are less 
exposed to accelerating wage growth. In addition, large differences in their income sources help explain the considerable 
variation in spending among this cohort. 
• The recently announced 3.6% Social Security cost-of-living adjustment could provide a meaningful boost in 2027. If inflation 
moderates as expected, benefit increases may outpace price growth, supporting older households' purchasing power and 
potentially narrowing their spending gap with other generations.

Older generations’ spending growth has lagged  
While total credit and debit card spending per household has picked up in 2026 in Bank of America internal data, a notable 
laggard has been the spending growth of older generations (Baby Boomers and Traditionalists). For around a year, discretionary 
spending growth for these older generations has been lower than that of overall households. This is in stark contrast to 2022-
2023, when they were significantly outstripping overall household spending growth (Exhibit 1).  
Exhibit 1: Discretionary spending growth has been slower among older (Baby Boomer and Traditionalist) households despite wealth effects 
Total credit and debit card spending per household on discretionary categories by older generations and overall (three-month average, % YoY)

Source: Bank of America internal data

On one level, this observation seems surprising given that in our piece on the older consumer we noted that a large and growing 
proportion of net worth is held by older cohorts (read: Entering the age of the older consumer). So what’s going on? One 
explanation is that wealth is very unevenly distributed even within the older cohort. Exhibit 2 uses the latest Federal Reserve 
(2025) Survey of Consumer Finances to illustrate that, across households older than 65 years of age, around two-thirds of net 
wealth is held by the wealthiest 10% of households. As a result, wealth gains from rising stock markets may not trickle too far 
down the wealth distribution of older consumers.  
Exhibit 2: The wealth distribution for older households is very skewed  
Distribution of net wealth for households over 65 years old (%)

Source: Federal Reserve Board of Governors (2025 Survey of Consumer Finances)  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Income and spending become more diverse and unequal with age 
Another important part of the story is that the rise in overall spending growth over the past year appears to be supported by 
labor market strength as well as rising wealth. The October Consumer Checkpoint described how a pickup in after-tax wage 
growth among lower- and middle-income households was likely a key factor driving a recovery in these cohorts’ spending growth 
(read: Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers). As households age, their income sources become more diverse (Exhibit 3), 
which means older generations will likely benefit less from this pickup in wage growth.  
Exhibit 3: As households age their sources of income become more diverse  
Sources of income by age (%)

Source: Federal Reserve Board of Governors (2025 Survey of Household Economics and Decision making) 
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

spending. For example, using Bank of America internal data, Exhibit 4 shows that households over age 62 that rely solely on

The 2027 COLA may offer some respite  
Still in our view there are some reasons for optimism around older generations spending outcomes.  
For some older households in the middle of the income distribution, tax policy is likely providing some support to their spending 
growth. The One Big Beautiful Bill Act introduced a temporary deduction of up to $6,000 for each taxpayer aged 65 or older for 
2025-2028, with the benefit phasing out above $75,000 of modified adjusted gross income for single filers and $150,000 for 
joint filers. This measure is likely lifting after-tax income and spending capacity for older households – particularly those in the 
middle of the income distribution.  
However, households on lower incomes may see less benefit from these policies as they likely already owe limited federal 
income tax. For these households relying on Social Security either as their sole or as a large component of their income, the 
forthcoming annual cost-of-living adjustment (COLA) to social security benefits will likely be a more important determinant of 
their budgets for 2027. The announced COLA for 2027 is a rise of 3.6% (Exhibit 7) – the largest since 2023, though lower than 
the 8.7% rise in 2022. 
Exhibit 7: The 2027 COLA increase is the largest since 2023 and close to the historical average  
Social Security cost of living adjustments (% increase as of January effective date)

Source: US Social Security Administration (SSA)   
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
The COLA increases are based on the year-over-year (YoY) % change between the third quarters of successive years in the 
Consumer Price Index for Urban Wage Earners and Clerical Workers (CPI-W) and are typically announced in October and effective 
the following January. If the COLA increase simply keeps benefits in line with inflation, why is it particularly interesting? For sure, 
many times it is not. But when inflation is close to a turning point, the increase can end up looking relatively high or low 
compared with inflation over the course of the following year. The 2022 COLA increase was a case in point, as inflation in the 
subsequent year slowed sharply. In our view, this was one reason older generations’ spending growth was stronger than that of 
other age cohorts over 2022-2023.   
Exhibit 8: Inflation is expected to decline next year 
Consumer Price Index (CPI) (% YoY), third quarter CPI-W increases (red dots, % YoY) and latest consensus CPI forecast for 2027 (dotted line, % YoY)

Source: Haver Analytics, Bloomberg. Some fall 2025 months might be missing due to the government shutdown.

Regarding the 2027 COLA announcement, there are some parallels with 2022, albeit on a smaller scale. In particular, the 
consensus expectation is that CPI inflation is likely to cool over 2027, largely reflecting the lessening impact of higher gasoline 
prices (Exhibit 8).  
So if these expectations prove correct, households that rely on Social Security may receive a more impactful boost to their 
budgets next year than they have for the last few, potentially narrowing the gap between older generations’ spending and other 
households.  
What categories of spending growth might benefit from this? It is hard to be sure, but notable growth deficits for older 
generations currently reside in discretionary areas like electronics, lodging and restaurants (Exhibit 9). Some of these may reflect 
the preferences of older households, but it could be that there is room for some narrowing in these areas.  
Exhibit 9: Large spending growth gaps for older households exist in a range of discretionary goods and services 
Difference between older generations’ (baby boomer and traditionalist) spending and overall households’ YoY spending growth by category (three-month 
moving average, percentage points)

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Store category aggregation for “more discretionary”, “partly discretionary”, and “mostly necessity” is defined as follows: 
More discretionary: furniture, electronics, building materials, hobbies, miscellaneous stores, nonstore retail, clothing. 
Partly discretionary: auto, health and personal care, general merchandise. 
Mostly necessity: Gasoline, food and beverage.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Bank of America Proprietary Market Landscape Insights Study is an online quantitative survey among Bank of America customers and 
noncustomers sampled and balanced to provide a representative view of the U.S. adult population. Insights are based on aggregated and 
anonymized responses to surveys. Significance testing is done at the 90 percent confidence interval. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
IMF ANNUAL MEETINGS
Resilience, but no relief. The IMF's latest global growth forecast is un-
changed from a year ago and from April 2026 (at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4%
in 2027), despite the energy shock, however with a different geogra -
phical breakdown: many energy importers and war-exposed countries
downgraded, and AI-exposed countries upgraded. Resilience was the

buzzword of the meetings, however it appeared mostly in the rear -
view mirror. Looking ahead, buffers are running thin, fiscal policy needs
urgent consolidation, and downside risks (from energy and AI exube -
rance) loom large. Global headline inflation is expected to rise to 4.7%
in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before easing back to 4.1% in
2027 (revised +0.2pp). Growth in advanced economies is revised up to
2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany), staying at 1.8% in 2027. Eu-

rozone growth is upgraded to 1.1% in 2026 (+0.2pp) and 1.2% in 2027.
US growth stays projected at 2.3% in 2026 and 2027 (+0.1pp). Germany
sees the largest G7 upgrade, to 1.3% in 2026 (+0.6pp) and 1.1% in 2027
(+0.1pp). France is the only major advanced economy downgraded, to
0.4% in 2026 (-0.2pp) and 0.7% in 2027 (-0.2pp). For more detailed
takeaways see Substack and this week’s EcoWeek editorial.
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Trade policy, competitiveness and budget agreements by year-end.

EU leaders asked the Commission to propose by year-end an instru -
ment allowing, in extreme cases, full closure of the single market to
a rival deemed to be attacking the EU economy. It is designed as a
“second-strike” deterrent against unfair practices and retaliation.
The Commission is also exploring extending the hybrid-vehicle safe -
guards approach agreed with China last week to other sectors such
as chemicals and machinery. On the "Made in Europe" label, vehicles

would need to be assembled in Europe with at least 70% of compo -
nent value (excluding batteries) sourced locally and with substantial
R&D in the EU. The UK would be included, provided London aligns with
EU trade defence measures. EU Council also called for decisive pro -
gress on the competitiveness agenda, with the single-market (inclu -
ding 26th regime) and simplification files to be wrapped up by year-end.
On the multi-annual financial framework, the objective remains a deal

by end-2026, so that legislation can be adopted in 2027.
EURO AREA
Moderate tightening does it. Multiple ECB Governing Council members,
speaking from the IMF Meetings in Bangkok, re-underwrote their
“moderate response” approach to the ongoing energy-driven infla -
tion surge. Accordingly, they signaled that additional rate action was
likely but not imminent, acknowledging that the rise in bond yields
was doing part of the job, and that there were no signs yet of second

round effects. Strengthening the case for moderation, the Eurozone in-
dustrial production fell in August for the second month running, by
0.5% m/m, pulled down by pharmaceutical output and motor vehicles.
That said, production in Netherlands surged to a new high (2.5% m/m
and 8.7% y/y), driven by AI machinery and equipment.
France
Business starts booming, insolvencies are stable, growth outlook is
downgraded. Business creations rose by 16% y/y in the 3 rd quarter

(+23% y/y in September), driven mainly by the digital sector and retail.
In parallel, business bankruptcies stabilised in the 3rd quarter according
to Altares, with a 0.5% increase y/y (compared with a 5.4% increase y/y
in Q2), and their impact on employment declined: 50,510 jobs at risk
in Q3, down 3% y/y (see our recent analysis). The Banque de France
halved its estimate for Q3 GDP to 0.1% q/q (same as Insee) due to
the manufacturing sector. Its monthly survey showed a sharp jump in

business uncertainty in September, driven by fuel prices and economic
deterioration (the most acute readings in industry and services since
April). The OAT-bund spread was rangebound (137-142).
Germany
Producer prices are rising. Wholesale prices rose 8.7% y/y (vs. 6.8% in
August) due to higher wholesale prices for energy products and inter -
mediate goods, particularly metals.
UNITED KINGDOM
Summer outperformance and kudos from IMF. Monthly GDP defied

expectations in August and posted a 0.1% m/m against a -0.1% consen-
sus. This marked a slowdown from July (0.4% m/m), but also a fourth
consecutive month of expansion. Industrial production underpinned
the advance: it accelerated to 0.8% m/m (consensus: -0.1%, prior: 0.3%)
thanks to pharmaceuticals. The IMF sees growth accelerating to 1.3%
in 2026 (+0.3 pp since July) and 1.2% in 2027. It also singled out the UK
among advanced economies for its efforts and progress in putting its

fiscal house in order.
UNITED STATES
Inflation heats up, manufacturing accelerates, but an October hike
is unlikely. CPI inflation exceeded expectations at 0.65% m/m in Sep -
tember (up 0.2pp), the fastest monthly pace since April. Accordingly,
the y/y rate climbed to 3.7% (up 0.3pp). The biggest upside surprise,
however, came from the core CPI: at 0.33% m/m (consensus: 0.2%, prior:
0.3%) and 2.6% y/y (consensus: 2.5%, prior: 2.4%); as services remained

sticky while goods accelerated to 0.3% m/m on autos and AI-related
items. Producer prices too rose well above expectations to 1.3% m/m
(consensus: 0.6%, prior: 0.5%) – the largest monthly increase since
March 2022 – and 6.3% y/y (consensus: 5.3%, prior: 5.6%). Industrial
production accelerated in September to 0.7% m/m (up 0.6pp) thanks
to utilities. Manufacturing returned to growth (0.3% m/m, up 0.5pp)
with gains broadening beyond the AI-related and aerospace sectors.

Retail sales excl. autos and gasoline, by contrast, slowed sharply to
0.2% m/m (down 0.9pp). During his speech at the IMF Meetings after
the hotter than expected inflation prints, Chair Warsh declined to hint
at an imminent rate hike. We continue to expect the next rate hike to
occur in December.
To watch this week:
- Developed markets Flash PMIs for October (Friday).
- Eurozone Germany September PPI, October ZEW (Tuesday), France Insee Business Climate (Thursday).

- UK August Employment data (Tuesday), September CPI and PPI (Wednesday), September Retail Sales (Friday).
- Japan September CPI, October PMI (Friday).

JAPAN
Producer price inflation eases. Producer prices fell for a second month
in a row in September, down a further 0.5% m/m, while the yearly rate
slowed for the first time since February (-0.8pp), though it remains
firm at 6.8%. The IMF forecasts growth at 0.7% in both 2026 and 2027,
broadly unchanged. The yen weakened past 158 per dollar again after

reports that the BoJ is leaning against a back-to-back October rate hike.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Emerging markets
Asia’s outlook upgraded, other regions downgraded.
The IMF upgraded its Asia growth forecast to 4.8% in 2026 and 4.4% in
2027. The largest upgrades are for economies well integrated into the
AI supply chain, notably Taiwan (+5.5pp to 10.7%), South Korea (+0.7pp
to 3.3%), Singapore, Vietnam, Malaysia and to some extent Thailand.
India is forecast to grow 7% in both 2026 and 2027 (fiscal year basis),

up 0.6 and 0.3pp from July on robust domestic demand.
Central Europe’s GDP growth is projected at 2.1% in 2026, slightly
lower than April’s forecast. In 2027, it remains unchanged at 2.3%.
Growth paths are diverse within the region.
In the Middle East & North Africa, GDP is expected to contract by 2%
in 2026. GCC real GDP should fall by 4.8% with only Oman expected
to post positive growth (4.1%). A marked rebound is expected for 2027
(MENA: 7%; GCC: 9.1%).

The growth outlook for Latin America remains subdued, revised down
to 2.3% in 2026 (from 2.4%) and 2.5% in 2027 (from 2.7%). However, in
its "International Trade Outlook 2026" published on 15 October, the
Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) ex-
pects the region to experience a trade boom in 2026: goods exports are
projected to surge by 20% in value terms (11% from price increases and
9% from volume growth), with trade to Asia (excluding China) soaring

by 32%, while intraregional trade growth lags behind at 8%. Yet, these
export gains are concentrated in commodities and low-value-added
manufacturing, limiting their spillover to broader growth. Stronger
trade is thus providing only a modest boost to the economy.
China
Explosive export growth, subdued inflation and credit growth. In Sep-
tember, export value growth surged to 36.3% y/y from 25% in August,
driven by semiconductors, computer equipment and other AI goods, as

well as green technologies. Export prices surged, especially for semi -
conductors, while total export volumes remained robust. Import value
growth also accelerated to 31% y/y from 28.2% in August, boosted by
both rising volume and rapidly increasing prices. The trade surplus
hit a new monthly record of USD 134 bn in September. CPI inflation
edged up to 0.9% y/y in September, mainly because of a 14.9% rise in
vehicle fuel prices. Food prices continued to fall (-1.7% y/y) and core

inflation was stable at 1%. Producer price inflation accelerated to 5%
(the highest since 2022) due to rising commodity prices. Growth in to-
tal domestic credit outstanding (“social financing”) continued to slow
down, reaching a record low of 7.1% y/y in September. Bank loans to
households declined (-2% y/y) for the seventh consecutive month.
In Q3 2026, real GDP growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y (1.2% q/q) from
4.3% y/y (0.9% q/q), primarily led by industrial production (which rose by

5.1% y/y on average in Q3 vs. 4.6% in Q2). On the demand side, exports
were the key driver while fixed-asset investment continued to contract.
Retail sales posted a small pickup in September but remained flat
in real terms in Q3 as a whole. With growth reaching 4.6% y/y year-to-
date, China needs only a 4.2% growth in Q4 to meet the official target of
4.5%-5% for this year – which seems easily feasible. The IMF trimmed
its growth forecast by 0.1pp to 4.5% in 2026 and 4% in 2027.

Indonesia
MSCI launched a consultation on reclassifying the country from Emer-
ging Market to Standalone Market status, with a final decision due by
end-June 2027, citing opaque shareholding structures and suspected
coordinating trading. Since January 2026, MSCI had frozen Index ad -
justments and threatened only a downgrade to Frontier market status
(like Pakistan), but market participants argued that a Frontier reclas -
sification would not properly reflect the extent of the market’s pro -

blems. Over the medium term, the risk is heavy foreign capital outflows
at a time when net FDI no longer covers the current account deficit.
This leaves Indonesia reliant on foreign portfolio inflows and the ru -
piah exposed to a reversal in sentiment. On the day of this announce -
ment, the Jakarta Composite Index fell by 11.6%, its steepest drop since
January 1998. Among Asian emerging markets, Indonesia has posted
the worst stock market performance since the beginning of the year

(-40.7%).
COMMODITIES
Iran/Oil. More of the same. Brent oil prices have remained broadly
stable this week (0.5% at 107$/b on Monday). Renewed Iranian at -
tacks on tankers in the Strait and intensification of Houthis’ attack over
the Saudi territory feed the geopolitical risk premium on barrel prices.
But IEA announcement of an accelerated 100 mb oil release (above
the March initial target of 400 mb) and reports that the US may lift
sanctions on the Russian and Iranian shadow fleets have contributed to

crude price stability. The spread with physical barrel (Dated Brent) has
declined (6.7$/b on Oct 16th). Trump administration’s various attempts
to lower diesel prices have had a limited impact for now. European gas
prices (TTF) recorded another weekly rise (4.7% w/w to 88.4 €/MWh,
the highest level since Dec 22nd, 2022) on the back of the rising number
of attacks on vessels in the Gulf and persisting disruption in the Strait.
However, EU gas inventories reached 74% of full capacities on October

16th (i.e., almost at the 80% month-end target), which should reduce
support for TTF prices going forward.
y/y = year-on-year
m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels

Weekly Economics
U.S. Week Ahead
Existing Home Sales • Thursday
Housing market conditions remain highly unfavorable. Mortgage
rates are the primary headwind, and they have climbed even higher
alongside the rise in longer-term yields. At the same time, low
supply is preventing a broad correction in home prices, leaving
prospective buyers squeezed on two fronts.
We expect that existing home sales slid 3.4% in August to an
annualized rate of 3.92 million. Behind this forecast is ongoing

weakness in mortgage applications and a recent bounce in
mortgage rates, which rose from 6.43% at the beginning of July to
6.66% by month-end.
We do not expect affordability conditions to meaningfully improve
in the near term. Supply is likely to remain constrained while high
mortgage rates discourage mobility. And mortgage rates are apt
to remain elevated amid firm economic growth expectations and
heavy government and corporate debt issuance.

Economics Week Ahead Economics
CPI • Friday
Headline inflation looks to have picked up last month. We estimate
the CPI rose 0.40% in August, with gasoline prices increasing a little
over 4% as renewed tensions in the Middle East pushed oil higher.
Grocery prices also likely rebounded after modestly declining in July.
Outside of food and energy, price pressures appear little changed.
We expect the core CPI to rise 0.23%, essentially matching July's

pace. Core goods inflation should remain firm, led by another solid
increase in used vehicle prices as consumer prices continue to
catch up to the trend indicated at wholesale auctions. Meantime,
household furnishings and other tariff-sensitive categories appear
to be stabilizing after declining earlier this year.
Core services inflation should be little changed with another 0.2%
monthly increase. But underneath the surface, we expect a modest
pickup in travel (led by lodging away from home) to offset slightly

cooler medical care and housing cost growth.
All told, we expect the report to show that while headline inflation
remains influenced by the ebbs and flows of the conflict in the
Middle East, core inflation remains contained. Should our forecast
prove correct, core CPI will ease to 2.4% year-over-year, in line with
its 6-month annualized rate but above the more recent 3-month
annualized pace of 1.7%.

### Page 4

Weekly Economics
G10 Week Ahead
European Central Bank Monetary Policy Meeting •
Thursday
We expect the European Central Bank (ECB) to raise the Deposit
Rate by 25 bps to 2.50% next week. August headline inflation
remained elevated at 3.3% year-over-year, though softer core
and services inflation suggest underlying pressures continue to
moderate. Still, the more hawkish tone of the July minutes and
renewed rise in energy prices should keep policymakers cautious.

The recent escalation in the Middle East has pushed fuel prices
at the pump close to their 2022 peaks, while natural gas storage
remains below historical levels, leaving the Eurozone vulnerable
to further energy price increases. The ECB is unlikely to respond
aggressively if the shock remains confined to energy given the
downside risks to growth. However, a broader rise in food inflation
linked to El Niño would be more concerning, as highly visible prices

could lift inflation expectations and generate second-round effects.
For now, resilient Q3 activity, elevated headline inflation and
contained wage growth support one final hike. The ECB will likely
retain and emphasize its scenario-based outlook in the updated
staff projections given continued uncertainty around the conflict
and energy prices. The policy path beyond September will depend
on whether energy and food pressures persist and begin to spread,

which could leave room for further tightening.

U.K. Monthly GDP • Friday
We expect UK GDP to rise 0.2% month-over-month in July, with
growth of 0.3% on a three-month-over-three-month basis.

Activity likely moderated after June’s strong rebound, though
services remained supportive as the July services PMI rose to 52.1.
Retail sales volumes fell 0.5% month-over-month, while softer
manufacturing activity likely reflected some reversal of earlier
precautionary stockbuilding.
The moderation appears more consistent with payback from
June than a broader deterioration in momentum. August PMIs
remain consistent with Q3 GDP growth of around 0.3% quarter-

over-quarter. Still, the renewed escalation in the Middle East and
associated rise in gas and energy prices adds upside risk to inflation
and could weigh on household demand. Against this backdrop and
with Bank of England hawks continuing to emphasize the need for
tighter policy, we expect the BoE to remain cautious and continue
to look for a rate hike in Q4. -1.0%

Economics Week Ahead Economics
EM Week Ahead
Mexico CPI • Wednesday
We expect Mexico’s August inflation rate to edge higher, with
headline CPI rising to 3.34% year-over-year (0.27% month-over-
month) from 3.26% year-over-year in early August. Part of the
pickup likely reflects less favorable base effects and a smaller
disinflationary contribution from food prices. While agricultural
prices continued to help restrain inflation in mid-August, the drag

was notably less pronounced than in July. Core inflation should
remain elevated at 3.98% year-over-year (0.25% month-over-
month).
While inflation remains within Banxico’s midpoint 3% target,
persistent core and services inflation suggest convergence toward
the midpoint of the target could prove gradual. For now, we
continue to expect Banxico to keep rates on hold, with the policy
rate remaining at 6.50% through the rest of 2026 and into early
2027 as policymakers await clearer evidence that underlying

inflation pressures are easing sustainably. While rate cuts remain
a possibility in 2027, the current inflation backdrop argues for
patience in the near term.

Brazil CPI • Friday
We expect Brazil’s August inflation rate to slow to 4.20% year-over-
year, helped by lower electricity, food and fuel prices. The main
driver should be a temporary reduction in residential electricity bills
stemming from a one-off credit, while recent data already point to
a notable easing in price pressures. Mid-August CPI came in weaker
than expected, with inflation falling to 4.24% year-over-year amid
declines in electricity, food, fuel and airfare prices. Beyond these

temporary factors, restrictive monetary policy continues to weigh
on economic activity, as evidenced by the recent slowdown in Q2
GDP growth. However, underlying inflation risks remain. A still-
tight labor market and government measures aimed at supporting
consumption could keep services inflation sticky, making progress
toward the 3.0% inflation target more gradual.
Overall, the inflation outlook remains consistent with additional
policy easing. We expect COPOM to deliver a 25 bps rate cut in

September, bringing the Selic Rate to 13.75%. Beyond September,
policymakers are likely to proceed cautiously as they assess
incoming inflation data and the impact of the election cycle on
inflation expectations. Our base case remains for a pause after
September, with additional easing more likely in 2027.

Document 305: Eurozone _ Growth on track despite the turbulence (2026-09-24)

### Page 1

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
GROWTH ON TRACK DESPITE THE TURBULENCE
Growth was stronger than expected in Q2 and is set to strengthen further in the coming quarters. According to our fore-
casts, it will reach 1.5% in 2027 after 1.1% in 2026. This growth is expected to be driven by investment and its knock-on
effect on exports. On an annual average basis, inflation is expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and 2.8% in 2027, peaking at

the end of 2026. The ECB is expected to continue its monetary tightening. At this stage, a single further rate rise, aimed at
keeping inflation expectations anchored, is expected in December. The impact of rising interest rates (key policy rates and
market rates) on public finances and private sector borrowing conditions will be worth monitoring.
GROWTH: BEYOND THE SHOCKS, A RESILIENT MOMENTUM
Growth in the Eurozone remained robust in the first half of 2026, at

0.25% q/q in Q1 and 0.33% q/q in Q2, excluding Ireland (where economic
activity is volatile). It was driven by Germany, where signs of a recovery
are becoming clearer (see Germany). Our baseline scenario forecasts
growth of 1.1% in 2026, followed by 1.5% in 2027 on an annual average
basis. This growth is expected to be driven by the strengthening of
trends already underway in public and private investment (particularly
in defence and AI) and in exports (trade in services and intra-zone trade

in goods). Household consumption is likely to be resilient too. According
to our forecasts, job creation would continue to underpin household
incomes, thereby cushioning the impact of inflation.
A NEW LEASE OF LIFE FOR INDUSTRY
The Eurozone is undergoing one of the most profound industrial trans-
formations in its history. This is giving rise to divergences between key
sectors but, above all, to new drivers of growth whose effects are beco-
ming increasingly apparent (see our analysis). Indeed, the signs of an

economic recovery are growing stronger. Business sentiment indicators
show a marked improvement in the economic outlook across most sec-
tors (Chart 2), driven primarily by electronics (AI, digitalisation), the
automotive sector and aerospace. European industry is thus benefi -
ting from strong demand for capital goods; a structural trend that will
continue to be fuelled by ongoing investment cycles in AI, defence and
electrification. The introduction of minimum European content quotas

in the draft law on the industrial accelerator, together with the Com -
mission’s intention to tighten the criteria for access to European public
procurement markets, will provide additional support for European in-
dustry in the longer term.
FOREIGN TRADE: A REPOSITIONING THAT IS PAYING OFF
Following a decline in 2026, linked to rising energy import costs, the
trade surplus is expected to rise to around 3.0% of GDP in 2027 (of
which 2% of GDP for goods and 1% for services). Growth in European ex-

ports has slowed compared with its pre-Covid trend and is being ham-
pered by the decline in Chinese and US markets. However, Eurozone
exports in volume rose significantly in Q2 (+3.4% q/q).
More importantly, since Liberation Day (April 2025), Eurozone coun -
tries have found significant sources of growth amongst other European
partners, which has enabled them to cushion the decline in exports to
certain third markets. This is evident in exports to the rest of the EU,

EFTA countries (Switzerland in particular) and, to a lesser extent, the
United Kingdom, Ukraine and the Balkans (Chart 3). The momentum of
intra-European trade therefore remains very strong. It is expected to
strengthen further with the anticipated investments in defence, AI and
electrification.
FINANCING: FINANCIAL CONDITIONS ARE EXPECTED TO REMAIN TIGHT
Financial conditions in the Eurozone remained tight in Q2 2026, accor-
ding to indices calculated by the ECB (including the BIG [Broad Inter -

mediation Gauge]). They are expected to tighten more significantly in
the second half of the year: i/ the rise in long-term bond yields, which
has accelerated since the outbreak of the conflict in Iran, is expected to
continue; and ii/ the ECB will continue its monetary tightening (+25 bps

world
in Q3, then in Q4). For the time being, the debt burden is still weighing
only moderately on public finances due to the long average maturity of
countries’ debt (8.2 years on average in the Eurozone in July), but this
burden will increase by 2030.
On the corporate front, the rise in interest rates has remained contained
(+2 bps since January 2026, reaching 3.9% in July), which has contri -

buted to the strong growth in new investment loans (cumulated over
one year, +9.1% y/y in July compared with 15.3% y/y in September 2025).
By contrast, cumulative net debt securities issues over one year re -
mained stable in July, whilst cumulative net issues of listed shares over
one year halved month on month.
Rising interest rates will continue to hamper new variable-rate
home loans to households (around 65% of new lending) more signi -
ficantly than fixed-rate loans, for which rates stood in July at 3.69%

(+4 bps m/m) and 3.36% (+2 bps m/m) respectively. New home loans,
which are stable in 2026 on a cumulative over one year basis, are there-
fore unlikely to see any improvement. A fall in real-estate transactions
could weigh on new consumer credit through a reduction in expenditure
on home furnishings and renovations, whereas consumer credit has
been fairly buoyant so far (year-to-date, +5.2% y/y in July compared
with +1.4% y/y in January).
LABOUR MARKET: RESILIENCE YET TO BE CONFIRMED

Employment in the Eurozone rose again by an average of 0.1% q/q in
the first half of 2026, driven by Spain and Portugal. Given the growth
outlook in these two countries, this momentum is likely to continue,
whilst business sentiment surveys in Germany suggest that employ -
ment is beginning to stabilise. Nevertheless, employment levels are
plateauing (France, the Netherlands) or even falling in other countries
(Austria, Finland), which is likely to slow the decline in the Eurozone

unemployment rate, meaning it is expected to remain close to current
levels in 2027. Furthermore, second-round effects of the rise in inflation
on wages cannot be ruled out: the ECB’s wage tracker suggests wage
growth of close to 3% by summer 2027 (2.9% y/y in June 2027, compared
with 2.6% in September).
INFLATION: A PEAK EXPECTED IN LATE 2026
Changes in wage dynamics will be a key factor in the inflation out -
look for 2027, whilst energy price rises are picking up again, driven

by the conflict in the Middle East. This rise is even sharper than in
the spring for gas (linked to restocking ahead of winter) and is likely
to feed through to electricity prices in several countries. We therefore
anticipate inflation peaking at 3.7% y/y in Q4 2026, before easing as the
energy component falls. On an annual average, headline inflation is
expected to stand at 3% in 2026 and then at 2.8% in 2027.
At this stage, the pass-through of rising energy prices to other com -

ponents of the consumer price index, particularly services, remains li -
mited. The alternative core indicators monitored by the ECB, including
the PCCI (Persistent and Common Component of Inflation, 2.1% in July),
remain close to the 2% target. Core inflation, however, is expected to
peak two months later than headline inflation (3% in March 2027).
Inflationary risks are nevertheless tilted to the upside. These are lin -
ked to food prices, which are expected to be affected by the energy

shock and weather conditions (summer drought, El Niño); they could
also stem from a delayed impact of energy price rises on underlying
inflation. The rise in prices of energy-dependent inputs (notably che -
micals and plastics) and those linked to the AI sector is expected to be
sustained. Growth, which we expect to remain robust over the coming
quarters, will also fuel underlying inflationary pressures.
MONETARY POLICY: TIGHTENING WITHOUT SLOWING (GROWTH)

Following two 25-basis-point rises (in June and September), we ex -
pect a third in December, which would bring the deposit facility rate
to 2.75%. It would then be in slightly restrictive territory (we estimate
the upper bound of the neutral rate to be 2.5%). This rise would be
aimed primarily at anchoring inflation expectations and, consequently,
at curbing the pass-through effects of inflation. Monetary tightening
would therefore remain limited. Indeed, the overshoot of the inflation

target would be temporary, and the operational framework adopted by
the ECB in June 2025 allows it to tolerate this, provided it remains brief.
However, it cannot be ruled out that the ECB might take further action
if the decline in inflation proves slower than expected.
PUBLIC FINANCES ARE CONTINGENT ON RISING INTEREST RATES
The fiscal impulse in the Eurozone is expected to be clearly positive in
2026 due to the rise in defence and infrastructure spending in Germany.

This will contribute to a widening of the country’s primary deficit (from
+0.9 pp of GDP to 2.8%, according to our forecasts). The improvement in
public finances is expected to stall in both France and Italy, at least in
2026, due to the impact of inflation on the debt burden via index-linked
bonds. This trend is expected to reverse in 2027 with less expansionary
fiscal policies. Nevertheless, the increase in the debt service burden
would fuel a further rise in the public deficit, which would thus reach

around 3.3% and 3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 2027 respectively (compared
with 2.9% in 2025). Against this backdrop, the Eurozone’s public debt-
to-GDP ratio is projected to exceed 90% of GDP in 2026 and reach 93%
of GDP in 2027, according to our forecasts.
Article completed on 15 September 2026

Document 307: Eurozone_ Improving business sentiment, driven by tech, is spreading to other sectors (2026-10-14)

EUROZONE: IMPROVING BUSINESS SENTIMENT, DRIVEN BY TECH, IS SPREADING TO OTHER SECTORS
Stéphane Colliac & Tarik Rharrab
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
The September PMI surveys confirm that growth in the Eurozone is getting stronger, boosted in particular by new export orders, which are
still rising. Sectors that were already performing well – technology and capital goods (including for defence) – are continuing to grow, while

those that had been struggling until now – aeronautics, consumer goods and financial services – are seeing their production indices recover.
This strong performance is particularly noteworthy, given that it has not yet been undermined by higher inflation.
The heatmap shows the PMI index readings: a reading above 50 indicates expansion, while a reading below 50 signals contraction compared to the previous
month. For each indicator, sectors are ranked in descending order based on their September 2026 reading.

Shades of red represent values above 50, while shades of blue represent values below 50: the intensity of the colour increases the further away they are
from this threshold.
Production growth spreads to more sectors
The PMI surveys on business sentiment confirm that business activity in the Eurozone is expanding at a faster pace and spreading to more sectors.
Household-consumption-related sectors (goods and services), which were adversely affected in the spring by the outbreak of the conflict in the Middle

East, do not appear to be negatively impacted by the rising inflation observed since August. The aeronautics sector, where production has mainly been
limited by supply constraints, is also showing signs of improvement.

Chart of the Week / 14 October 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
These trends, although still moderate, come alongside a drive to upgrade equipment, fuelled by investment in digital technology, electrification
(which is supporting the automotive sector) and defence. The momentum in technology goods and services further strengthened in September.
The production index for computer and electronics hit a five-year high, while the software services production index stayed close to its July peak.

Financial services are also enjoying a significant improvement, reflecting the healthy lending momentum despite interest rates starting to rise
(see our analysis).
Growth driven by exports
Exports drove growth in the first half of 2026, both across the Eurozone as a whole (see our analysis) and among its key members (notably
Germany). This momentum is also increasing, as indicated by the ‘new export orders’ component of the various PMI indices. As was the case with

production, there has been a particularly noteworthy increase for capital goods (technology and machinery) and related services, particularly digital
services. This trend is playing out more broadly, as, in September, new export orders rose across all sub-sectors, with the exception of intermediate
goods and consumer goods, where contraction recently eased. There has been a significant improvement in the transport sector, particularly in
the aeronautics industry, which has major markets in Asia. Recent data on French exports show that aeronautics exports to this region are rising

once again.
The convergence between rising production and increasing export-order backlogs shows that Eurozone growth is based on an increasingly solid
foundation. Inflationary pressures are not holding it back for the time being.

► 美國9月CPI年增率上升至 3.7%，核心CPI年
增率由 2.4%升至2.6%，核心月增率 0.3%，
高於預期值0.2%。能源是通膨反彈的主因 ，
貢獻整體 CPI月增幅超過一半；能源衝擊雖
為主導 ，但消費韌性使企業保有成本轉嫁能
力，住房與部分服務價格維持黏性 ，需求面
通膨略有提高 ，加上企業資本支出提升經濟
與企業獲利韌性，使總需求不易快速降溫。
► 凱投預期Fed 12月將升息1碼，明年3月再升
1碼，政策利率可能在 4%以上維持更久 。受
到油價高盤 、通膨僵固 ，與財政惡化 、政府
發債籌資逢企業債搶錢等影響 ，美歐日主要
國家公 債利 率近 期 來到高 位 。其中 ， 法國
2027年財政預算欲刪減支出(包括養老金、公
部門工資等)引發民眾反彈 ，衝擊主權信用評
等，法國CDS與法德10年期公債利差擴大 、
歐元走弱 ，美元轉強 ，商品、黃金承壓 。法
國政治 不確定性讓 歐股修正 、法股最疲 ，成
熟國家公債殖利率升高 ，增加企業借貸成本

與股市評價壓力，續為金融市場擾動因子。

► 美國第三季財報季展開 ，市場將觀察企業在
高利率環境下的獲利表現 。根據FactSet數據
顯示 ，標普500指數第三季獲利 成長預估達
29.5%，顯示企業基本面仍具支撐 ，其中科
技 產業 受 惠AI 需求帶動 ， 獲 利 成 長預 估達
65%，持續扮演企業獲利的重要成長動能。
► 聚焦科技類股 ，AI資本支出仍為本輪企業獲
利成長的主要驅動力 。高盛指出 ，第三季AI

基礎建設相關企業將貢獻標普 500獲利增長
的54%。此外，大型CSP之雲端業務營收有
望加速成長 ，反映AI運算及雲端服務需求持
續提升，有助支撐後續資本支出擴張動能。
► 近期市場波動仍受高利率環境影響 ，壓抑投
資人風險偏好與估值擴張空間 ，顯示市場目
前壓力更多來自評價面而非基本面 。隨財報
數據陸續公布 ，若企業持續繳出優於預期的
獲利表現 ，並維持正向財測 ，市場有望逐步
回歸基本面評價 ，使企業獲利成長逐步支撐
股市表現。
美企財報獲利穩健，對股市中長期有撐

評價面疑慮下，股價難以進一步擴張

投資焦點
► 醫療保健 、醫療服務 與生物技術等行業 ，在
景氣不好時 ，市場預期民眾對醫療保健產業
的需求 不易 下降 ， 成為傳 統防禦 型類 股代
表。目前金融環境利率偏高 ，11月美國總統
大選國會恐變天 ，股市波動風險上升 ，觀察
過去當 VIX恐慌指數上揚時 ，醫療保健產業
相較於科技股更穩健，具有更好抗震能力。
► 醫療與生技類股近一年漲幅明顯 ，主因1)生
技股過去一段時間受到高利率 、融資成本及
臨床試驗風險壓抑 ，部分公司估值偏低 。當
臨床數據改善 、或導入AI篩選資料與 藥物，
投資人重新評價未來現金流與價值 。2)大型
藥廠面臨既有藥品專利到期壓力 ，透過收購
取得新藥與研發管線 ，提高有潛力的生技公
司併購價值 ，帶動整體產業估值回升 。3)肥
胖、癌症與慢性病用藥創造長期市場 需求，
創新藥物 (如瘦瘦針 )讓部分醫療公司成長不
再只依賴人口老化，高盛今年7月將全球減肥
藥市場 2030年銷售額預估上調 15%至1,140
億美元，看好此類藥品的長期商業潛力。

醫療生技近年表現佳，估今年3Q醫院客流量年增4%VIX恐慌指數上升時，健護醫療表現相較穩健
市場波動上升、醫療保健表現佳，獲利穩定成長

內部參考文件請勿外流
► 美國醫療費用深受法令影響 ，由於商業雇主
贊助保險 (ESI)和平價醫療法案 (ACA)的自付
額增加 ，以及最高自付費用要求 ，較去年同
期提高 ，使今年第三季美國整體住院人數 、
手術量和醫院客流量均呈現改善 。高盛預估
今年第三季醫院客流量年增4%，加上季節性
因素，下半年醫療保健利用率將較上半年成
長，整體第四季預估獲利穩健 ，甚至可延至
明年，反應在近期XLV ETF股價創新高。
► 展望2027年，各醫療保險計畫普遍優先考慮
透過提高會員保費 ，和要求費用分攤(尤其是
提高會員自付費用上限 )來擴大利潤空間 ，並
持續優化覆蓋範圍和產品組合 ，預估商業雇
主提供的醫療保險 (ESI)保費趨勢，將在明年
加速成長至過去 20 年來的最高水準 。 以
Refinitiv預估明年美國各產業獲利成長來看，
醫療保健和生技相較於今年獲利明顯增加 ，
反而今年獲利極佳的能源和通訊受高基期影
響，獲利增速下滑 。投資人在整體金融環境
波動放大時期，宜以醫療生技股穩中求利。

投資焦點
近3個月漲幅和預估獲利呈正成長，醫療生技股佳
醫療保健和生技預估獲利明年明顯成長，有利股價續揚
資料來源：Bloomberg，凱基投顧，凱基證券整理
預估今明年產業獲利差異，醫療保健和生技成長最大

股市
 美股第三季獲利年增率預估為雙位數成長 ，市場靜待財報結果與展望 ，和對AI資本支出回收影響 。
考量地緣衝突 、殖利率偏高、評價面壓力與鄰近美國期中選舉 ，短期金融市場紛擾不止 ，可增加防
禦股增強耐震度 。長期AI、科技發展趨勢不變 ，逢股市拉回時可分批布局 ，分散配置於不同國家與
產業，降低市場整體波動，亦可以槓鈴策略(防禦+成長)穩中求利。
 歐、日股可作為分散配置重點 ，全球利率走揚有利銀行股 。此外，地緣衝突頻傳 ，歐洲國防軍工仍
有投資題材。
策略風格：可增加防禦股配置，中長期看
好AI題材如科技及半導體，非AI部分關注
醫療保健生技產業
區域：日本銀行股、歐洲金融、歐洲國防
債市
 全球公債殖利率攀升 ，預估今年底前主要國家仍有升息空間 ，殖利率偏高環境使 短中期債券提供吸
引力，逢利率走高、鎖利領息。高信評債體質較佳 ，具穩定現金流與還款能力 ，聚焦風險調整後利
差具吸引力者，包含金融、科技、通訊及公用事業等產業。
債種：中短天期高信評債鎖利，產業如金

融、科技、通訊及公用事業等
匯市
 歐洲政治風險上升、歐元短期弱盤，美元高檔有撐。
 日本銀行再度升息時間不定，日圓區間震盪。

 中東地緣衝突短期難解，油價維持高位但震盪加劇。

人買盤依舊，提供黃金中長期穩定支撐。
